## D173 — Daoris extends agents' collaboration as a Collaboration Fabric: the owner's unified proposal accepted as written (2026-10-16)

**Decision (the owner's).** The owner brought in `docs/DAORIS_UNIFIED_COLLABORATION_ARCHITECTURE_AND_PLAN_2026-10-16.md`
and, asked how to settle its six architecture decisions (§12, ADR-A to ADR-F), chose *"Accept the proposal as written"*
on 2026-10-16, and said why: *"what we have right now is working, but I can see the limitation, so we need a better
foundation."* So, as that proposal states them:

- **ADR-A.** A Collaboration Fabric is the first-class substrate; a deterministic scheduler, a DAG or a workflow is an
  optional pattern over it, never the core ontology (§2). Quest, Chain, Workflow, Session, Helper, Ask and the driver
  are adapters and projections over it, kept with their meanings (§8: no second authoritative Quest or Session state).
- **ADR-B.** `Participant` and `RuntimeBinding` identity (§3), with explicit visibility levels, opaque to recoverable
  (§4), and partial visibility shown truthfully.
- **ADR-C.** The capability transport is MCP first, with a native adapter where a harness needs one, for exact supported
  harness versions, measured (§4).
- **ADR-D.** Messages are durable, correlated and deduplicated, and say queued, acknowledged, delivered and acted upon
  apart (§6), compatible with the existing sources of truth.
- **ADR-E.** Long-lived responsibility outlives the agents that act on it, with explicit handoff (§3 `Commitment`, §4).
- **ADR-F.** Credentials and permissions are scoped per participant: a descendant inherits no key, publishing right or
  account secret; the gateway enforces grants at the executor, never by model text (§7).

The proposal's principles (§1), phases and exit criteria (§10), ten canaries (§11) and review checklist (§14) are the
contract its work is accepted against. Its §9 stands: correctness work stays first, and the fabric's work runs beside it
in isolated branches. Per §12, the backlog holds only the C0–C2 slice; C3–C7 wait for contract tests to justify them.

### Guards for the build (a second opinion, verified against the code; they shape the build, they do not reverse the decision)

- **The fabric is the foundation; today's pieces move onto it, one concept at a time, with one source of truth each.**
  The owner chose a new foundation over growing today's pieces further. Daoris already has early forms of its parts: the
  knowledge connector's MCP tools (ADR-C's transport), an ask's channel and its entries (D167; ADR-D's records), quests
  with D163's hand-over and helpers (ADR-E's responsibility; the helper as one collaboration), the state pipeline
  (D169). C0 maps each primitive onto what exists and names which store becomes canonical; where a fabric primitive
  takes a concept over, it becomes that concept's store, with a mapping and a migration, and the old surface becomes
  its adapter (§8). Never two authoritative stores for one concept at once, and never a second mutation path beside the
  shared handlers (§14; D171 point 3).
- **A child's identity is issued, not inherited.** A native subagent that inherits its parent's environment would call
  as the parent (the connector reads `DAORIS_SESSION_ID`): a distinct restricted binding, issued locally, before a caller
  counts as a connected child (ADR-B, ADR-F). Reading keeps D107's rules; a change to Daoris keeps D89 and D170's
  proposal-and-Apply.
- **Measured, per harness and version.** Whether a native child inherits MCP servers and rules, carries a trustworthy
  identifier, and what a parent's stop does to it, is observed with real child-originated calls on Claude Code and
  Codex, never assumed (§4, §11). The owner approved small probes on their accounts on 2026-10-16.
- **The first slice stays C0–C2.** §10's illustrative vertical slice (a helper, a cross-tree message, an artifact, a
  delegated action) reaches C3–C4; the first rows prove binding and read-only access only.

### Rejected

- **One canonical execution graph or a scheduler that assigns all agent work** (the proposal's superseded table): agents
  negotiate, delegate natively and coordinate themselves; a graph or DAG is an opt-in plan, never the core.
- **Only Daoris-spawned root agents as participants**: harness-native subagents and their descendants are connectable.
- **Assuming every harness gives the same subagent telemetry**: capability and visibility levels, degraded honestly.
- **Holding ADR-A and growing today's pieces instead** (the second opinion's recommendation): the owner chose the new
  foundation, because what works today shows its limits.
- **Holding all six decisions until later**: offered, and not chosen.

### Supersedes

- `docs/DAORIS_FUTURE_DIRECTIONS.md`, now the proposal's Appendix A, and the proposal's own earlier sources, as its §0
  says: where they conflict, §0–§14 take precedence.
- D65's "no other engine" stands in this sense: the fabric is a substrate of interoperability, not a competing workflow
  engine (§9's revised interpretation).

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-C0, the contract audit, with both harnesses measured).** The matrix and receipts are
`docs/2026-10-16-fabric-contract-audit.md`. Each existing concept has one store, and the fabric's primitives map onto
them: messages onto the channel and a helper's files, commitments onto quests and hand-over, references onto knowledge ids,
authority onto proposal-and-Apply and the handed rules. The one store missing is a native child's binding to its part.

The second guard's hazard is observed, not inferred. On Claude Code 2.1.299 a native subagent, and its own subagent at
depth 2, call through the parent's very connection. On Codex 0.160.0 each thread starts its own server process, but from
the parent's entry. On both, the server sees the parent's `DAORIS_SESSION_ID`. What tells calls apart is the harness's own
lineage, never the environment. On Claude that is the `PreToolUse` hook's `agent_id`, joined to the call's
`_meta.claudecode/toolUseId`. On Codex it is `_meta.x-codex-turn-metadata`, which names the thread, its parent and the
spawn kind, and also the workspace's absolute path. Both harnesses hand the child the parent's allow and deny. Killing the
root's process alone ended the in-process child and terminated the servers, with no cancel and no child-end signal.

A Claude agent defined with its own `mcpServers` got a separate server with Daoris's environment, but it kept the parent's
server too. In one run, two siblings shared that one process, and it closed when the first sibling stopped. So it is no
per-child credential.

Codex refused every MCP call by default until the server's tools were approved, and `--ephemeral` broke its spawn.

C1 and C2 are go. The binding is observed and keyed to the existing part (C1a). A child's calls are read-only by their
lineage, never by an argument, with an unreadable lineage treated as a child (C2a). The install's real doors, ACP for both
and codex-acp's approvals, are C2b's to measure.

Rejected by the audit:

- **The environment as a child's identity.** Both harnesses give the child the parent's.
- **Claude's agent-scoped server as a per-child credential.** It is per type and shared by siblings, and its lifetime is
  the first sibling's.
- **An identity a tool's arguments carry.** The model writes those; the harness writes `_meta`.
- **A new session record per child.** C1a binds a child to the part it serves (§8: no second Session state).

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-PROBE1, the probe kit tracked).** C0's scratch kit is rebuilt as `tools/fabric-probe/`. It has
the stand-in server, which logs every message synchronously (`server.mjs`), and the hook logger (`hook.mjs`). The launchers
(`launch.mjs`) keep a run in `_fixtures/fabric-probe/<run>/` and start every process with the run's own `DAORIS_HOME`, the
session's `CLAUDE*` variables and every inherited `DAORIS_*` variable dropped. The receipt (`receipt.mjs`) prints ids and
never a path. `tools/fabric-probe.test.mjs` holds them without a harness. Its plain run on Claude Code 2.1.299
(`claude-sonnet-5-5`, scenario `child`: 39 s, 4 turns, 0.089 USD) reproduced run A. One server process and one `initialize`
served the root and its subagent, both under the root's `DAORIS_SESSION_ID`, and the hook's `tool_use_id` met each call's
`_meta` toolUseId, naming the subagent's `agent_id`. It also showed where a Claude child's parent is written: the stream's
`task_started` names the agent by `task_id`, the same id as the hooks' `agent_id`, beside the `tool_use_id` of the `Agent`
call that started it, and that call's `PreToolUse` names its caller.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-C1a, a native child bound to its part).** The connector reads each call's `_meta` before its
tool runs (`CallLineageFilter`). It appends what the harness wrote there, ids only, to the session's lineage file,
`<home>/sessions/<id>/lineage.jsonl`, for a session the driver named. The driver hands Claude Code a lineage hook beside
its rules (`lineage-hook.mjs`, on the connector's calls and on each subagent's start and stop). It reads Claude Code's
stream for each subagent's start, parent and end, and appends the part's end when its run lets go. `ChildBindings` folds
those lines into the part's children: harness, native id, parent and depth, the signals that named each, connected or
observed, and active, finished, ended or lost. A child gets no session record, and the part's record stays canonical. The
file is the one new store; its twins are in `.claude/knowledge/twins.md`. What no harness said stays unknown: a parent
nobody wrote, a call with no lineage, a Claude call no hook named. Nothing is restricted yet; that is C2a's.

The receipts are from 2026-10-16, through `tools/fabric-probe/` with this branch's connector and hook.
`ChildLineageReceiptTests` holds the files the runs wrote.

- **Claude Code 2.1.299** (`claude-sonnet-5-5`, scenario `family`: 66 s, 5 turns, 0.156 USD). Two siblings and a grandchild
  under the first were each bound with a parent and a depth, from the stream's `parent_task_id`. Each call was joined to
  its agent by `tool_use_id`, 4 of 4, and each child finished by its own `SubagentStop`.
- **Codex 0.160.0** (`gpt-6.1-sol`, effort low, scenario `family`: 69 s, 263 463 input and 518 output tokens). The same
  family was bound from `x-codex-turn-metadata` alone. Four connector processes, one a thread, appended to one file and
  lost no line. Codex says no thread's end, so each child ends with the part and is never `finished`.
- **Claude Code, scenario `kill`.** The root process alone was killed 24 s in, while its child's call was held open; the
  killed root wrote no result, so usage was not measured. No `SubagentStop` and no cancel came, and the part's unclean end
  marks the child `lost`.

Canary 1 holds: the hook decides nothing, and every call answered as before. Canary 2 holds: a grandchild is placed under
its parent where the harness surfaced one, and a child with no known parent has none. Canary 9 holds: a signal a harness
lacks stays unknown or partial, as Codex's missing types and ends show.

Not measured: either harness over ACP, so whether `_meta` and the hook reach Daoris's protocol doors (C2b's), and a Codex
kill (the audit's run G saw no end signal). The driver's own path (the hook from `SpawnSettings`, the stream's watch, the
end) is held by its tests, not yet by a session an installed driver ran.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-C2a settled before it is built, with a second maker's opinion).** The scout found that the
row, as written, would refuse a root whose lineage the connector cannot read. On the doors where lineage is not yet
measured (either harness over ACP, the owner's daily door), that would refuse roots the mutations they use. Codex,
asked, held to point C0's rule, and its reasons are adopted:

1. **Fail closed, for every session the connector names.** A restricted tool runs only for a caller proven a root: a
   Codex call with a valid thread, source `user`, an absent parent and no contradicting child signal; a Claude call
   whose tool-use id joins a hook line that names no agent. Missing, malformed, conflicting or unjoined lineage is
   refused, and is never read as a root. An id that is malformed is kept as malformed, not dropped to look absent,
   in `CallLineage` and in the hook (with the driver's fold as its twin). A connector naming no session (a person's
   own agent) keeps today's behaviour, as a scope this guard does not protect.
2. **Staging waits on a measurement, not on a bypass.** The ACP doors are measured before C2a reaches the install:
   C1a's lineage file, written by the owner's own sessions on the build that carries it, shows whether an ACP root's
   calls join. If they do not, the door is made to carry lineage. An unprotected ACP door would be the owner's
   choice, recorded as an amendment here, never a default.
3. **The class table** covers every registered tool, derived from the registration, so a tool added later is refused
   to a child until it is classed.
   - Read: `knowledge_search`, `knowledge_get`, `knowledge_repositories`, `knowledge_convergence`, `registry` and
     `quest_list`.
   - Refused to a child: the row's six mutations, `channel_read` (it moves the parent's mark), `helper_answer`,
     `opinion_answer`, `review_serve`, `review_ready`, every `*_propose` and `knowledge_refresh`.
   - `quest_publish` is the one fixed exception (the plan's §4: a subagent may ask another repository), in its plain
     shape only: no attachments, never published as the session's ask, and no review or workflow proposals.
   - `daoris.me` is not a tool yet, and is not part of this row.
   - No grant of a mutation to a child in this row.
4. **The refusal** comes from the filter, before the tool runs, as an error result. It says that nothing was done, and
   why: the caller is a subagent, or its lineage could not be verified (the second never claims the first). It says
   that the reads remain, and that the request belongs in the subagent's result for its parent, or in a quest.
5. **The Claude join** reads the session's file from its end, bounded in bytes and time and cancellable, matches the
   exact tool-use id, and refuses on a conflicting match or a timeout. Its budget is a starting value until measured.
   The rehearsal's stand-in sends a genuine root lineage and asserts the error flag; its `review_ready` justifies no
   bypass.

This is enforcement on calls a harness mediates, not authentication against code that runs as the same user: a
child that can start processes could start another connector. That residual is named here for C2b and the grants.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (the end of a piece of work, the owner's direction).** Asked how cleanup works today, and told that no
watcher reads git (the watch's look judges every session branch each poll, a platform plugin is asked a pull request's state,
ending a conversation removes its tree, and *Accept* makes the landing branch), the owner: *"because the end cycle is
different depending on the workspace and its config, so if we have a git watcher as the end, we should clarify this as the
new design of the fabric"*. What it sets for the fabric's design:

1. **When a piece of work ends is one fabric event, not several paths.** Today the end is read in four places that each
   judge it alone: the look's tidy, the plugin's pull-request query, a conversation's end, and *Accept*'s landing. The
   fabric names one end for each piece of work, and those places read it.
2. **The end is observed in git**, by a watcher over the repositories that hold Daoris's work (their refs, and the
   remote's state as a fetch or the platform's plugin reports it), not inferred from a session's state or a press.
3. **What counts as ended is the workspace's and the repository's end cycle**, read from their configuration (the commit
   rule, the landing rule, the platform plugin's pull request), so one workspace ends at a merge on the line, another at a
   pull request completed, another at *Accept*'s branch pushed.
4. **Design first** (FABRIC-END1): the event, the watcher, each configured end cycle, and what reads the end (cleanup, the
   page's *Accept* and its foot, a quest's close, the record). It is checked against the C0 audit's stores and D88, D102,
   D148 and D164 before anything is built.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (events over polling, the owner's direction).** On the end of work, the owner went on: *"so this should
be an event using a watcher instead of polling every 15 s? I think we have a lot of polling logic that can be improved"*.
What it sets:

1. **What the machine can say when it changes is an event, not a poll.** Git's refs, a worktree's head and Daoris's own
   records, request folders and locks are files: a watcher hears them change, with no process started (process starts are
   slow on this machine, under antivirus).
2. **A watcher is backed by a slow reconciliation**, never trusted alone: on Windows a watcher drops events under churn (a
   buffer overflow during an install) and git writes through lock files and renames. The reconciliation is minutes apart,
   not seconds.
3. **What only a server knows** (a pull request completed on a platform) comes from the platform's webhook where a
   reachable service can take it, else from a fetch that is asked for when something suggests it is due, not on a clock.
4. **Design first** (FABRIC-EVENT1): an inventory of every poll in Daoris (the loop's look, the page's queries, the account
   readings, the knowledge server's freshness, the request folders, the host's checks) with each one's interval, what it
   waits for, its cost, and the event that could replace it. FABRIC-END1's end of work is its first consumer.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-EVENT1 and FABRIC-END1, the design: proposed, revised on a second maker's opinion, pending
the owner's yes).** The design is `docs/2026-10-16-fabric-events-design.md`: the inventory of 28 waits, the shape, the
probe's numbers, the cycles, the rejected shapes, the canaries, seventeen rows and five questions for the owner. Nothing is
built. Codex reviewed it, supported the direction and asked for revisions before any build; each point was checked against
the code and adopted. It proposes:

1. **An event is a wake, never a fact, and never the whole look** (§A3). A source says a topic and a subject; it is
   debounced (250 ms quiet, 2 s cap, a coalescing and not a barrier) into its topic's due set, which a judge drains
   atomically, one judgement per subject at a time, a hint arriving meanwhile kept for the next. A hint never runs today's
   look, which syncs with the remote before it plans (Driver.cs:332). Each topic has a reconciliation deadline; a failed
   read leaves its subject unknown and tried again. The judge writes its one store, and the page hears `STATE_CHANGED`
   through D169's publisher.
2. **The sources** (§A4): one `ChangeWatch` primitive in the driver library outside `Domain`, with spelled-out recovery
   (generations, reopen, an absent or replaced root, a resume) and bounds; a watcher per repository's common git dir,
   never per tree, and on the home's record folders; the local service's long-poll, `GET /api/changes`, sampled from a
   dedicated connection's `data_version` and answering families by their own revisions, never the database, since the
   look's own sync pass rewrites a row each time (QuestSync.cs:91); a fetch at moments that say it is due, never on a
   clock.
3. **Measured first, on this machine under its antivirus** (§A5): a watcher on a repository's common git dir heard 16 of
   16 ref writes before git exited, renames included, with no process started. One run shows it can work here, not that
   it keeps working: overflow, sleep, long holds, handles and a fetch are the build rows' to measure.
4. **The end of a piece of work** (Part B): the unit is the tree's branch; the words are *claim* (the agent's done, never
   the end), *handed* and *ended* (the line holds the committed tip, proven by git). The cycle is resolved as *Accept*
   resolves it, a named workflow's bound landing included (TreeLands.cs:346-350); no `end` key. The ref watcher wakes one
   end judgement per repository that moved, with the 5 min tidy kept as the reconciliation. Auto-landing stays on the
   look until the ledger, config and gate wake it too.
5. **The store** (§B5): `Ended { At, By, Tip, Line, On, OnAt, Repository }` on the `landings.json` entry and on the
   `session-branches.json` entry, its codes the clean-up's own. An end stops holding when its tip, its line's commit, its
   cycle's line or its repository changes. An ended session branch's entry is kept as a trace on both forget paths
   (BranchReplays.cs:432, TreeRemovals.cs:206). Ends are written only by the process holding the home's lease as its loop.
   The ledger keeps the claim, and the service is unchanged.
6. **The readers** (§B6) take the end from the store: the look's automatic tidy, the page and *Accept*'s foot, the CLI,
   Ask Daoris and a quest's close (shown beside the claim, never changing the ledger). The person's own removals (the
   sweep's press, LAND3's tidy) take handed work that is not ended, and are not moved to the end.
7. **The owner's direction overrides** the scout's *no watcher yet*, on the measurement. D147:62 (a timed fetch), D148's
   timer rejection, D88:150-153 (landed only on another branch stays) and OutputTail's *polled, not watched* stand. The
   fetch-when-due moments would amend D148 point 2's list of occasions (§Q5). Both slow clocks stay until the watchers'
   recovery is measured.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (the fabric events design accepted, with the owner's amendments).** Asked to accept
`docs/2026-10-16-fabric-events-design.md` and three of its questions, the owner accepted it and started its building, and
answered the three in their own words, which amend it:

1. **On whether a push may count as done:** *"so we can define a clear end of work: each repo or workspace might have a
   different cycle of work"*. The end of work is **defined** per workspace and repository, not only derived from the
   landing rule: a cycle names the steps its work passes (for example claimed, handed, pushed, its pull request
   completed, on the line), and which step is its end. The proofs stay: a step is reached only on git's or a plugin's
   evidence, and a removal still needs the work held where it cannot be lost.
2. **On how the server's end is learned:** *"so this will be a plugin; with an extra plugin we can define a different
   end for the fabric"*. A plugin may contribute a step or an end to a cycle, and how it learns it (its own watch, its
   platform's hook, a query) is the plugin's. The fabric takes the plugin's event, and its evidence, as any other event.
3. **On the slow clocks:** *"so instead of using a 15 s [clock] we should build them into the event system, so the
   fabric can define a different event as its chain"*. Fixed clocks are not the core: a timer is one event source among
   the others, and the fabric defines events and their **chains** (an event that leads to the next, such as the refs
   moving, the end judged, the work tidied, the quest closed), each chain the workspace's to configure.

The design is revised with a part on the event chain, the cycle's definition and plugin-defined events, before the rows
that depend on them. The first two rows, the change watch (FABRIC-EVENT2) and the end judgement extracted (FABRIC-END2a),
stand under every answer and are built now.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-CHAIN1, the design's part C: proposed, revised on a second maker's opinion, pending the
owner's yes).** Part C of `docs/2026-10-16-fabric-events-design.md` answers the three amendments above, and its §C1 says
what it changes in parts A and B. Nothing is built. Codex reviewed it and asked for revisions before acceptance; each
point was checked against the code and adopted, and its two choices are left to the owner (§Q1, §Q2). It proposes:

1. **A cycle per workspace and repository** (§C2). Its steps are `claimed`, `handed`, `pushed`, `pull-request`, `line` and a
   plugin's declared steps. Its end is any of them but `claimed`, each reached only on git's or a plugin's evidence. It is
   set as a `cycle` on the landing rule: D164's inheritance comes with it, `driverconfig.ts` is its twin, and its Setup row
   is *Work ends*. A named workflow's `end` governs where one is chosen, as D157 §2.3 places such fields; every named
   workflow holds a landing (WorkflowNamed.cs:491), and an older version reads `line`. A repository with no rule lands by
   merge (Landing.cs:979-984) under the default cycle, `line`, which is today's behaviour exactly. A cycle that names what
   its rule cannot reach is refused at the door that sets it.
2. **The end apart from the removal** (§C3). *Held*, the line's proof, alone lets the look's automatic tidy go. An end
   defined before the line never allows a removal by itself (D88:150-153 stands). An end not yet reached is *pending*,
   *unreadable* or *unreachable*, said apart. Whether the tidy waits for an end after the line is the owner's (§Q1); a
   deadline, if any, counts from `Held.Since`, which is persisted.
3. **A plugin's step** (§C4). The manifest declares only the plugin's own `steps` (both twins); `pushed` and
   `pull-request` stay built in. There is a loop point, `work/watch`, and a fifth kind of point, the event `work/step`: a
   real change to a wire that ignores notifications today (Hooks.cs:349-351). Its sender is the process Daoris started,
   never a field. It is believed only for a unit the process was told of, bound to that unit's tip, and for a step a
   rule names. It is bounded in bytes, rate and queue, replayed after a drop on a `resync` frame, kept as the plugin's
   word, and never removes anything. How the plugin learns the step is its own.
4. **Events and chains** (§C5, §C6). The event types are part A's sources, the end's facts, the plugins' events and
   `timer`: each topic's reconciliation deadline expires as an event among the others. Fact links fire on a change; the
   tidy is an effect link, pending until done, retried on the wakes that can unblock it and recovered after a crash. A
   removed unit becomes a trace, which is never judged again, so a chain converges. Whether a workspace may configure
   more than its end, from a bounded menu, is the owner's (§Q2).
5. **One store per fact** (§C7). Part B's `Ended` is kept as `Held`, a plugin's own steps as `Steps` (at most two per
   step), and a built-in step's event writes the existing `PullRequestState`. *Ended* is derived at read, never stored.
   A chain's in-flight state is memory only, rebuilt by the start's reconciliation.

Twenty-seven rows follow, the two dispatched first. Four questions stay with the owner: whether the tidy waits for an end
after the line; whether chains go beyond the end; whether the end reaches the quest's record; and the plugin's new due
occasions.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (part C accepted, with the owner's answers).** Asked §Q's questions on the design's part C, the owner
accepted it and answered:

1. **Whether the automatic tidy waits for an end defined after the line:** *"it can be set as configuration, so a true
   'end' means cleaning up everything unless it's the result, so we can set up different tasks in the fabric, including
   some tasks that require human steps"*. So:
   - the wait is the cycle's to configure, not one policy;
   - the cycle's end, when reached, is the point at which everything the work made goes **except its result** (the work
     the cycle names as its result: the landed branch, the pull request, what a plugin's step reports);
   - a cycle may hold **human steps**, steps a person reaches by a press, beside git's and a plugin's.

   The safety rules stand: nothing goes before the line holds the work, and a removal still judges again before it goes.
2. **How far a workspace configures its chains:** the bounded menu (FABRIC-CHAIN3): typed events, effects chosen from a
   fixed list that never remove or write anything, and no scripts.
3. **A plugin asked at the new moments:** yes, within D148's minute, with no redundant fetch and never at a removing press.
4. **Part C:** accepted and built, its rows dispatched in order. The human step, and "the end cleans up everything except
   the result", amend §C2 and §C3 before CYCLE1a is built.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-CYCLE0, human steps and the end's cleanup in the design: proposed, pending a second maker's
read).** §C2 and §C3 of `docs/2026-10-16-fabric-events-design.md` take the owner's answer above. Nothing is built.

1. **A human step** (§C2) is a step a person reaches by a press, `{ "human": id, "label": …, "after": … }` in the cycle's
   ordered `steps`, its id `human/<id>`. A cycle may read: on the line, a person's *QA done*, a plugin's *deployed*, the
   end.
   - **Who presses it:** only the person at D156's doors: the window, a terminal confirmed in the window over RPC1's pipe,
     or a keyed terminal with no window. Never an agent or a plugin.
   - **Where:** on the work's cycle line, in What needs you, and at its terminal twin, offered once the steps before it
     are reached.
   - **What it keeps:** who, by which door, when, at which tip, and its label as written.
   - **When it lapses:** with its tip, by §B5's rules.

   *Ended* now means the end and every step the cycle orders before it, reached at the current tip.
2. **The result and the true end** (§C3). *Held* has two forms: on the line (the default), or, for an end before the
   line, in the cycle's result, proven by git. The result is never taken:
   - the landed work on the line;
   - the pull request's or pushed landing branch, until the line holds it;
   - the landing branch for `handed`;
   - a ref a plugin step names.

   At a true end everything else the work made goes: the session's tree (moved aside and purged at its fourteen days, as
   today), its session branches, and the landing's other branches. Never taken: the person's branches, anything not
   Daoris's, the recovery refs, and whatever a guard keeps. A cycle ending before the line so amends D88:150-153 for
   itself; the default cycle keeps it exactly.
3. **The wait is the cycle's** (`wait`): `held` (the default, today's), `end` (until the end or the person's
   *Clean up now*), or `end` with `days` counted from `Held.Since`. An end after the line that is never reached goes by
   that bound or that press.

The rows FABRIC-CYCLE1a, FABRIC-END4a and FABRIC-END4b change, and FABRIC-HUMAN1 is added before FABRIC-END4b. One
question stays with the owner, the end on the quest's record, and no row needs it before FABRIC-END5.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-EVENT2, the change watch built, the requests folder its first source).** `ChangeWatch`, in the
driver library's `Platform/`, is the first event source, and general: a topic names its judge, its deadline and how many
subjects it judges at once; a root names a folder, its topic and subject, and the filter of what it hears. A hint waits
250 ms of quiet, capped at 2 s, then joins its topic's due set, which a drain takes in one swap, so a hint during a
judgement is judged after it, never beside it. Every subject is judged at its topic's deadline whatever was heard, and a
judgement that fails is tried again with a back-off inside it. Recovery follows the design's §A4.1. A callback from an old
generation is dropped, and a loss judges its root at once and reopens it, backing off and then left to the deadline after
three tries in a minute. An absent root is tried at each reconciliation, and a folder replaced at its path is found by its
file id there. A wait that overran by 30 s counts as a resume. At most 32 roots are watched. Each source sits behind
`IChangeSource`: `FolderWatcherSource` on the machine, and a fake in the tests that raises moves, losses and nothing.
`SessionRequestWatch`'s 1 s timer is gone: the requests folder wakes its look, a 3 s listing stands behind it, and its filter
hears no take, answer or deletion, so a look wakes no look. `DriverWatch.Nudge(topic, subject)` joins the plain `Nudge()`,
which still means everything, and each look writes `watch.looked` with its ms and what woke it. The watch's judgements write
`change.judged`, an idle deadline excepted (the machine log design §4). Measured once on this machine, its antivirus on: a
say was answered 333 ms after it was written, and eight watchers held one handle each and about 1.3 KB of nonpaged memory
(10,752 bytes for eight), all given back at dispose. The fast tests hold canaries 1-3; `ChangeWatchProcessTests` holds the
real watcher. The hint still runs the whole look; splitting the look by topic is FABRIC-EVENT3a.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-END2a, the end judgement extracted).** `Trees/SessionTrees/EndJudgement.cs` gathers §B3's cycles
and proofs behind one answer, `EndState` (claim, handed, ended, unknown) with the proof's code, where it held, the commit and
the line judged. `Judge` is pure over `EndFacts`; `GatherAsync` reads the facts through the proofs' own code
(`LandedProofs`, `ContentHolds`, `PullRequestAsks.VerdictAsync`, `TreeFollows.UnlandedAsync`) from a `RefSnapshot`, git being
asked as those proofs ask it for what a snapshot does not hold. The cycle is an input (`EndCycle`): `Accepts` resolves it as
*Accept* does (`TreeLands.cs`:346-350, a named workflow's landing first), `Recorded` from a landing's plugin, pull request,
`Squashed` and workflow, so the defined cycles of FABRIC-CHAIN1 plug in where a cycle is resolved. No store, reader or caller
changed: each keeps its own judgement until END2b and END4 move it. Three readings of §B3 are stated: the merge form and no
landing share their proofs, and the line's origin copy ends them as its local branch does (§B2 defines the line so, over the
table's *local line*); content ends every branch form, the squash one included, as the clean-up does today; and a pull
request's answer is a proof only where the cycle has a plugin. Gaps found: `ContentHolds` answers null for both *not held* and
*could not say*, so an unread content proof reads as not held until it says otherwise (the proof's own change, not this row's).
Canary 6's cases are in `EndJudgementTests` and, over real git, `EndJudgementGitTests` (a `[gone]` upstream, a commit held
only by patch under a changed file, a completed answer whose merge commit is not here).

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-CHAIN2, the chains built on the change watch).** `Platform/Chain.cs` holds the design's §C6 as a
ranked table, `ChainTable.Fixed`, and the `Chain` that runs it on `ChangeWatch`'s topic queue. Each topic's wake is its
event (`home` for the requests folder), and its deadline is a `timer` event naming it, so a timer is one source among the
others. A topic that only hints feed keeps its deadline through `ChangeWatch.Subject`. A wake runs the links that hear it in
rank order, each once, and what a link emits is heard only by the links above it. The table refuses a link that emits what a
link at or below its rank hears, and so refuses a link to itself. It also refuses a reader that emits, and an effect without
a deadline or the start among its wakes. An emission outside its row is dropped at run time too. Links come in three kinds.
A fact link (1, 3, 4) gets what it heard and answers what changed. An effect (2) asks its seam, `IChainEffect`, for its
pending units, read from the persisted facts. A unit its facts name is tried, and a wake that can unblock the effect tries
every pending unit. The unblocking wakes are `ledger`, `press`, `home`, `timer(refs)` and the start. Each unit is tried once
per wake. On a wake of deadlines alone, a unit is tried at most once per deadline. A refusal or a throw leaves the unit
pending. Links 5-7 are readers: a topic's own judge, which emits nothing into the chain. Two words are added to §C5:
`start`, which `RecoverAsync` says once with every topic's timer, so each topic is judged whole and each pending effect is
found; and `ChainPending.From`, the first moment a unit may be tried, counted by the effect from a persisted fact
(`Held.Since`). The chain keeps no origin of its own, and a restart neither resets nor shortens the wait. Only which unit was
tried when is held in memory. The machine log writes `chain.fired` (link, topic, count), `chain.pending` (`refused`,
`failed`, `recovered`) and `chain.dropped` (`undeclared`). Only link 7 is wired, on the requests folder, the one file source
built. Links 1-6 keep their rows and seams unbound, and an unbound row is skipped: END2b and END3 bind 1, END4a binds 2 and
calls `RecoverAsync` at the loop's start, PLUGSTEP1b binds 3, EVENT3a binds 5 and END6 binds 6. Three choices are left to
those rows. First, `SessionRequestWatch` owns its watch and its chain, so whether a loop runs one chain over every topic is
EVENT3a's or END4a's to settle. Second, link 4's publish must never come back as a `press`: a `press` is a route answering
someone's press. Third, link 2 hears `home` by type, so every request also retries a refused tidy, once, until `home` is
told by its file. The fast tests on the fake source hold canaries 4, 11, 12 (every end §C2 allows, against the table), 16,
17 and 18. A link pointed to a lower rank fails `ChainTests`, and `SessionRequestChangeTests` proves link 7.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (the owner's goal over the fabric's design).** The owner, after part C: *"so all my input is kind of
'requirements', but if you have any better idea you can always update it; what I'm thinking of is a system that's
easier to work with plugins and new steps, maximizing the automation we can have"*. So:

1. **The owner's words in these notes are requirements, and the goal they serve is named:** a fabric in which plugins and
   new steps are easy to add, and in which as much as possible runs by itself. A design choice is weighed against that
   goal, and a better shape is proposed rather than the requirement followed to the letter where they differ.
2. **What the design takes from it, proposed for the next revision** (a second maker's read, then the owner's yes):
   - **a step may be performed, not only observed:** when it falls due, Daoris asks the step's owner (a plugin) to do it,
     then waits for its event;
   - **one contract for every step,** built in, human or a plugin's: an id, an order, how it is reached, its evidence,
     and optionally how it is performed, so that a new step is a plugin's declaration and needs no change to Daoris;
   - **automatic by default:** a cycle with no human step runs to its end by itself, with the person's steps only where
     configured;
   - **an easy step plugin:** the kit scaffolds one, and `plugins try` drives it through a simulated cycle;
   - **plugins subscribe to the fabric's events** through the bounded menu, so automation is added without new chain code.

**Note, 2026-10-16 (the design's part D, toward the owner's goal: proposed, revised on a second maker's review, pending
the owner's yes).** Under the owner's goal (*"a system that's easier to work with plugins and new steps, maximizing the automation
we can have"*), Codex checked parts A-C. It found the branch lifecycle had become almost the whole fabric, with thin human
steps, a fixed git set as "everything", no plugin task or cleanup contract, an unspecified menu and plumbing first. It then
proposed part D. Part D of `docs/2026-10-16-fabric-events-design.md` takes that proposal with each claim checked against
the code, and governs where it conflicts with A-C (§D1). Nothing is built. It proposes:

1. **Scope and a visible milestone** (§D0). Branch cycles are the fabric's first adapter. Every unit shows its end
   reached, its result kept, its cleanup pending (and why) or completed, and its recovery retained. This is built first,
   for the default cycle.
2. **The work unit** (§D2). An adapter interface over the canonical records, never a work database. Branch work is first
   (a stable id on the session branch's entry, referenced by landings), then quest, ask and the delegated task.
   Deployments, documents and plugin artifacts stay results or resources until a store holds them. Sessions execute;
   they do not own.
3. **The ownership inventory** (§D3). An entry per resource, derived from the existing records, with receipts only for
   what a plugin created. Every entry gets a disposition, and an unknown owner blocks the cleanup. Daoris removes only
   what its executors own; a plugin cleans only what it created, through `work/cleanup`, by an opaque id.
4. **One step contract** (§D4) for built-in, human and plugin steps. Steps are observed, performed or pressed. Evidence is
   bound to the unit, its revision, the step's version, its inputs and its prerequisites, and a changed dependency
   invalidates what follows. Timeouts are persisted `timer.step` deadlines.
5. **Human tasks** (§D5): a brief, an attachment schema, approve or reject with a reason, at D156's doors only.
6. **Performed steps** (§D6): `work/perform` on the supervised watch connection, its dispatch persisted before sending,
   one idempotency key per attempt, and the capability `work.perform@1`. This amends §C7: attempts survive a restart. A
   performer's limits are listed.
7. **Automatic by default** (§D7). Unconfigured, part C's default exactly. Configured, every eligible automatic step
   runs, and a human task or a missing authority stops it visibly. A step grants no authority, and production stays the
   person's.
8. **The menu, completed** (§D8): typed transitions; the effects `notifyPerson`, `deliverPluginEvent`,
   `requestReconcile` and `offerPersonAction`, none of which writes; Setup's *Work* pages and `daoris-driver work`.
9. **The plugin author's path** (§D9) and **the collaboration fit** (§D10), with ACP1 → C2a → C2b as their own gate, then
   COLLAB1 and DELEGATE1.
10. **The rows, re-cut** (§D12). The collaboration gate (ACP1, C2a, C2b, then COLLAB1 and DELEGATE1) runs in parallel
    from now. The default lifecycle and its milestone come first, with the default inventory derived in MILESTONE1a, and
    the responsiveness rows move forward. UNIT1a-d split the adapter model, the step contract, the cycle's
    configuration and the manifest twins. PERFORM1a-e split transport and supervision, durable execution, evidence
    authorization, resource cleanup and the kit. HUMAN1 follows UNIT1b and UNIT1c, apart from the PERFORM rows.

Codex reviewed the revision. It found the proposal carried faithfully and its deliberate changes improvements, and
asked for one more round. That round adds:

- **the performer's contract the kit promises** (§D6): `idempotent`, `reconcile` and `transient` declared in `perform`;
  outputs as opaque refs, resolved only from a current acceptance; and `work/reconcile`'s frames and answers;
- **the trust model** (§D6): plugins are trusted code, started on the person's account and told the home
  (HookProcess.cs:83-99), so a performer's limits are protocol obligations, not isolation, and FABRIC-ISOLATE1 is named
  for a later design;
- **a branch's removal apart from the unit's retirement** (§D3, §C6): an open unit is never pruned as a trace;
- **resource accountability through invalidation**, with a durable, idempotent `work/cleanup` (§D3);
- **a termination argument for the menu's responses** (§D8): transition identities, judges as functions of the world,
  attempts that only Daoris, the person or a new revision create, and a finite measure;
- **corrections:** the evidence door records a verdict only on a done awaiting evidence (QuestExchange.cs:1529-1540);
  a delegated task is a quest another repository took (D69), not D163's hand-over in one tree.

Two questions go to the owner (§D13): whether reaching the end closes the quest by itself (recommended: offer the
person's own done, since automatic closure would be a new contract), and which kind of work comes second (recommended:
the delegated task).

**Note, 2026-10-16 (FABRIC-EVENT2b, 2c and 2d, the change watch's three loose ends).** A terminal's words a loop keeps now
wake its look by their topic: `LoopWords.Nudge` takes the session and `DriverWatch.Said(session)` is
`Nudge(requests, session)`, which the headless host and the shell's `Words` both call, so `watch.looked` says the words woke
it. `SessionRequestWatch` makes `sessions/requests/` before its watch starts, which both loops build there, so the watcher
opens at once and the first request after an install is heard rather than found at the 3 s listing. The desktop shell hears
`SystemEvents.PowerModeChanged` (Resume) and raises `PowerResume`, a seam in Modules a test raises by hand, which the
loop's requests watch hears to reopen its roots and judge them; the 30 s overrun rule stays as the fallback, and the
headless host, which hears no power event, keeps only that.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (part D accepted, with the owner's amendments: nodes and wires).** Asked to accept part D, the owner
answered, spelling fixed:

1. *"each step can have its own state, so we don't have any difference between different types of steps, so they can
   link and communicate normally; and I think the rest is fine"*. Part D is accepted. Every step, of whatever kind (built
   in, human, a plugin's), carries one and the same state model, so any step can follow, feed or wait on any other in the
   same way.
2. On whether a quest closes itself at its work's end: *"close automatically can be a step to set"*. Closing the quest is
   a step a workspace may add to its cycle, not a fixed behaviour; the quest's contract (D46) is amended only where a
   cycle holds that step.
3. On which kind of work comes second: *"so waiting for another task can be a step too (and we can name those steps
   better, more like nodes? and we have different ways to wire them)"*. Waiting on another piece of work is a step; steps
   are named **nodes**; and the way nodes connect is a **wire**, of more than one kind.

So the cycle becomes a graph of nodes joined by wires. A revision of the design (nodes, their one state model, the kinds
of wire, the quest's close as a node, and waiting on another unit as a node) is worked through with a second maker before
the rows it changes are built. The rows it leaves alone, the events and the default cycle's facts, go on now.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (part E accepted; what the asker sees; the names).** The owner accepted part E (nodes and wires),
with the quest's closing details finished with a second maker (the design's §E5 and its D173 note). They chose, for a
graph that closes its quest itself, that the asker sees *submitted* once the agent's result is in, then the current gate
or hold, then *done*. On the names, asked whether to use node and wire, node and edge, or a naming in the manner of
修仙 like the project's own name, the owner chose plain words in both languages, saying *"let's just do this
normally"*: **Graph / 图, Node / 节点, Wire / 连线, Chain / 链, Done / 结束, Result / 成果**. The code and the design keep
the same technical names, and the glossary holds the pairs.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (FABRIC-EVENT3a, the look's topics).** A wake no longer runs the whole look. `Driver.Part`
(`LookPart`) names the part a look runs:

- **`look`** is the whole look: the remote sync, then the lost-claim stop, then the local plan, exactly as before. It runs
  at the loop's start, at the `look` topic's deadline, after a failed look's pace, and on a plain `Nudge()`, which still
  means everything.
- **`plan`** is the local plan, every other part of the look: the plugins, the rule proposals, the moved lines, the set-up
  plans, the snapshot, the hand-overs, the channel, the reviews, the opinions, the helpers, the plan and its starts, the
  intakes, the endings and the landings. It runs on a topic's wake (`Nudge(topic, subject)`: `ledger`, a terminal's words
  by `requests`) and on a session's end.

A wake therefore skips no judgement the whole look would have made. The claims the lost-claim stop asks about move only
when the sync runs, so the wake defers the sync and the stop together to the deadline. One sync stays in `plan`: the sync
after an ending the look reports (the sync design §8). It is counted by endings, not by wakes, and it lets the remote hear
a done as soon as before.

The deadline is `pollSeconds` after the last whole look ended, measured whatever woke the looks between, so a stream of
wakes never holds back the reconciliation or its sync. The remote sync's own deadline is that same `look` deadline today.
Giving the pull its own interval (the inventory's 60-120 s) waits for canary 10's measurement (FABRIC-EVENT7).

The look is link 5 of §C6, bound on the loop's own `Chain`. A whole look fires `timer(look)`; any other look fires a
`ledger` event that keeps its topic and subject, and link 5 hears both. `chain.fired` is written for a plan that started
something. This settles CHAIN2's first open choice for link 5: `DriverWatch` owns the loop's chain, and `SessionRequestWatch`
keeps its own chain for link 7. The loop stays the `look` topic's queue, since its wait hears a session's end, and so the
`ChangeWatch` under its chain is never started. Wakes taken during a look are still one look after it. A plain nudge is
remembered apart from the last wake, so a topic's wake that follows it cannot turn the look it asked for into a plan.
`ChangeTopics.Ledger` names the change stream's topic for FABRIC-EVENT3b.

`watch.looked` gains `part` (`look` or `plan`). `DriverWatchTopicsTests` holds canary 4 (a thousand wakes, the ledger's
and a terminal's, run no remote sync), a `ledger` wake that takes a quest an agent published without the sync, the
whole look at its deadline under a stream of wakes, and a session's end that syncs once, after its ending. The planner's
and the tick's tests are unchanged, and both loops (the headless host's and the desktop's) run `DriverWatch`.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (FABRIC-EVENT4, the home's record files wake their topics).** `Platform/HomeWatch.cs` puts the home's files
on the change watch as the chain's link 7 (`home` events, `timer(<topic>)` deadlines; `ChainTable.Fixed`'s read row now hears
each topic's timer). The topics, named in `ChangeTopics`: `config` (`driver.json`, wherever the config's resolution put it),
`plugins` (a plugin folder arriving, leaving or changing its `plugin.json`, never `.data`), `lines` (`lines-moved.json`) and
`words` (the held-words file), each woken into `DriverWatch.Nudge(topic, subject)`, so the look runs its **plan** part, never
the remote sync; and `update` (the staged build's `build.json` and the swap journal under `update/`, and the request file), the
installing shell's `InstallUpdater`, which pokes its own look.

- **One judge, a stamp.** A hint or a deadline reads the topic's files' stamps (length and last write) afresh and compares them
  with the last read: a hint whose stamps did not move, and a deadline that finds nothing, wake nothing, so the reconciliation
  is no look. Each topic's deadline is `HomeWatch.Deadline` (30 s), the updater's 5 s, since a staged folder that did not exist
  when its watch opened is found by the reopen at the deadline. Each topic's existing read stays as it was: the look reads the
  config, the plugins and the lines at each look, so `pollSeconds` still reconciles them.
- **Its own writes wake nothing (EVENT2's rule).** A writer calls `HomeWrites.Mine(path)` after it writes, which marks the file's
  stamp; a judge that finds the marked stamp wakes nothing, and a later move by anyone else, a terminal's, has another stamp and is
  heard. Marked: `RegistryFollowing.Moved` (`lines-moved.json`), the held words' save and removal (`SessionWords.Save`),
  `InstallUpdate.Write` and `StagedBuild.WriteJournal`. `driver.json` has no marked writer: its edits are the person's, through
  routes that nudge, so a config edit wakes one more plan look within the debounce.
- **The updater.** `Pace` (2 s) is now only the drain's: while an update drains, it waits on sessions no file says ended. With
  nothing staged it looks every `Idle` (30 s), and at once when its folder or request moves. `InstallUpdater`'s tests are
  unchanged; `UpdateWatchTests` holds the staged build heard, found at its deadline when silent, and its own request waking nothing.
- **Both loops.** `DriverWatch.RunAsync` owns the watch, so the headless host and the desktop's loop hear the same files; the
  shell's power resume reopens it (`DriverWatch.Resumed`). A held-words file's wake runs the plan, which reads no held words
  itself: `SessionWords` holds them in memory and keeps its own 30 s pump, so the topic's use is the look the person's clear
  (`HeldWordsFile.Forget`) or another process's write is owed, and a pump kicked by the file is left to a later row.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (FABRIC-EVENT5a and 5b, Ask Daoris's and Settings' polls become publishes).** Three changes the driver makes
with no press behind them are now told to every window as `STATE_CHANGED`, in a press's words (`StatePublisher.Publish`), and the
three page reads that waited on a clock wait for them. D169 §4 is unchanged: the event is the shell's, and a plain browser has none.

- **Words taken up** (row 23, `sessions`). `DriverLoop.ComeUpAsync` hears `ServiceClient.Moved`, which every `AdvanceAsync` raises,
  and tells `sessions` for the record at every move: to `working` where `ChatGoOn` takes an ended conversation's kept words up (the
  person's say, a terminal's, or the shell's come-up), Ask Daoris's included, and out of it, which includes the restore of an ended
  record where every queued word was withdrawn, which announces no `SESSION_ENDED`. The page's `GOING_ON_POLL` (1.5 s) falls to the
  reconciliation below.
- **A proposal written** (row 24, `help`). The connector writes it in another process, so `HomeWatch.ForProposals` hears the
  proposals folder as the `help` topic (`ChangeTopics.Help`, `HomePart.Named`): the folder's `*.json` names alone, so a file arriving
  or leaving moves it, and a proposal settled, a card's units kept (`Drawn`) or a claim written is no new proposal, which is why no
  writer marks its own write and a `HELP_PROPOSALS` read that draws a card cannot wake itself. **The window's own process runs this
  watch, started before the home's lease is waited on**, so a headless loop that drives the home leaves a window's Ask Daoris as
  told as when it drives itself; it tells `help` once at its start (a proposal that arrived before the watch opened) and at each
  arrival, and runs no look. Its deadline (30 s, link 7) is the reconciliation on the driver's side.
- **A size landed** (row 22, `wiring`, a new scope after `browser`). A walk keeps one reading, as it ends (`WiringSizes.MeasureNow`),
  so a size lands once per walk, never entry by entry: `SYSTEM_WIRING { measure }` tells `wiring` as the walk starts, and again once
  `Finished` completes, which is after the walk has left the home's list, so the read it tells says it is no longer measuring. The
  2 s read in `useSystemWiring` goes. Telling entry by entry would need partial readings kept as it walks, which no page row asks.
- **The page** (`bridge/published.ts`). `pollUnlessTold(hears, ms)` is `RECONCILE_MS` (**30 s**) where a bus tells and `ms` where
  none does; the three intervals above pass through it (the proposals while a conversation is live, the going-on list while words
  wait, the wiring while a walk is measuring), so a window with no bus keeps its poll exactly (1.5 s, 4 s, 2 s) and a desktop window
  reads at most every 30 s beside the events. A desktop window drops the fast intervals from the start, with no first event to wait
  for: its page is served by the shell that publishes (one build, unless a host is chosen explicitly), and a wire's words are added
  to. These queries are `enabled: isAvailable` already (the wiring and Ask Daoris are shell-only), so the browser's poll is held by
  the function's own test, not by a view that runs there.
- **A change told during a read is not lost** (`stateHub.ts`, `askAgain`). A query with no data yet has nothing to cancel, so an
  invalidation reused the read in flight, whose answer pre-dated the change and whose success cleared the invalidation. The hub
  now asks once more after a read that was on its way, for every scope. **A late measure answer** (`useSystemWiring.measure`) is
  kept only where no read newer than its request landed, so it cannot put `measuring` back over the walk's end.
- **A publish missed** costs a bounded wait, never a stale page for good: the 30 s read above, a mount or a focus read (the
  queries' `staleTime`), the hub's reads at each press and tick, and the proposals folder judged at its deadline. The bridge replays
  none, so the slow read is what covers a delivery that failed. A window opened after a publish reads at its mount.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (FABRIC-END2, the default cycle's facts kept).** The design's §B5, §C7 and §D2-§D3 for the default cycle.
Nodes are not built here.

- **The facts.** Both entries now carry `Held { At, Since, By, Tip, Line, On, OnAt, Repository }` (`Trees/HeldFact.cs`): the
  session branch's in `session-branches.json` and the landing's in `landings.json`. `Repository` is the `.git` folder's
  identity (`FolderIdentity`, as the change watch reads it). A tree's opening writes the branch unit's id once
  (`GrownBranch.Unit`). Every later record of the branch keeps it, and *Accept* names it in the landing's `Units`, on the
  landing and on each advance. *Ended* is not stored, and no reader reads these facts yet (MILESTONE1a will).
- **The writer.** `EndKeeping` (`Trees/SessionTrees/EndKeeping.cs`) judges each standing landing and session branch of a
  repository through `EndJudgement`. It writes `Held` where the judgement proves an end. A fact that still holds is not
  written again. Where a fact lapses, its branch is judged anew and the fact is written again or cleared. It runs in the
  look's tidy, before anything goes (`AutoTidies`). It runs in *Accept*, before the rule's tidy (`TreeLands`). The tidy's
  own judgement and removals are unchanged.
- **Only the lease's holder writes.** `HomeLease.HeldHere` is true where the home's lease names this process: the
  instance's loop and its window's presses, or a verb acting alone under the lease it took. A verb beside an instance
  holds nothing, so it writes no `Held`. Canary 9's structure test holds this: only `EndKeeping` calls the records'
  `Hold`, and only `LandedBranches` sets `PullRequestState`.
- **The cross-process writer is a lock on the two files.** `RecordLock` is a lock file under `locks/records/`, held alone
  for each read, change and write. It is taken inside the store's in-process gate, by the loop and by every verb alike,
  and is waited for for up to 10 s. **Why the lock and not RPC1b now:**
  - The lost write is a race on two files. A lock in the two record classes closes it for every writer, the verbs
    included, at once.
  - RPC1b would move every trees verb onto the instance's pipe to close the same race. That is a larger change to the
    verbs' contract, and it would still leave a verb alone and the migration writing the files.
  - With the lock, RPC1b is a choice about who acts, no longer what keeps the files whole.
  - The rule that only the lease's holder writes `Held` stands beside the lock: the lock keeps each write whole, and the
    rule keeps one judge.
- **Lapse, on standing branches only (canary 7).** A fact lapses when the tip moves, when the cycle names another line,
  when `On` no longer descends from `OnAt`, or when the `.git` folder is another. A trace is never judged again.
  Three readings of the design are stated here:
  1. `At` is the last judgement that wrote the fact, so a look that changes nothing writes nothing.
  2. `Since` is kept while the tip, the line and the repository are the ones it was first held at.
  3. A lapsed fact whose new judgement is unknown is cleared: read as not known, never as ended.
- **Traces, never pruning an open unit.** Both forget paths (`BranchReplays.ForgetGoneAsync` and `TreeRemovals.Forget`) go
  through `SessionBranches.Forget`. A held entry is kept as a trace (`GoneAt`). An entry whose unit a standing landing
  still carries is kept too: the line does not hold that landing yet. Such a trace is an **open** unit (§D3), and its
  retirement is derived at read: its landing is held or gone. Other entries are forgotten, as before.
  - A repository keeps its newest 50 retired traces. Open ones are never pruned nor counted.
  - `All()` and `Of()` read standing entries only, as `LandedBranches` does. `Entries()` reads the traces too.
- **Proof:**
  - `HeldFactsTests` (fast): old entries read as not yet judged, the round trip, `Record` keeping the unit and the fact,
    traces and open units on forget, the pruning, canary 7's pure half, `Since`, the lock waited for and timing out, no
    lease writing nothing, and canary 9's structure.
  - `HeldFactsGitTests` (Process, Git): canary 7 over real git (the line rewound, the tip moved, another line, the
    repository replaced at its path). A trace survives the person's removal and the look's tidy and is not judged
    again. *Accept*'s tidy keeps an ended trace under the merge form and an open unit under the branch form, retired by
    its landing's end.
  - `RecordLockProcessTests` (Process): a second process's read, change and write under the lock serialize with this
    one's, and neither write is lost.
- **Left for later rows:**
  - The keeper re-judges every branch not held at the tidy's pace, beside the tidy's own judgement: more git starts per
    pace, not yet measured (canary 10, END3). END4a's candidates from the stored ends remove the duplicate.
  - The chain's link 1 stays unbound until END3 binds it.
  - No machine-log line marks a write.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (the design's part E, nodes and wires: proposed, revised on a second maker's review, pending the
owner's yes).**
Codex proposed part E from the owner's three answers above. Part E of `docs/2026-10-16-fabric-events-design.md` takes it
with each claim checked against D157's workflow code, `QuestExchange` and D69, and governs where it conflicts with A-D
(§E1). Nothing is built. It proposes:

1. **One lifecycle for every node** (§E2): blocked, ready, active, then reached, failed, rejected or unknown, plus
   skipped, stopped and invalidated, with the retries spelled out. Only evidence authority differs by kind: who may
   report a reach, a failure or a rejection (a rejection only by a person at D156's doors). A `wait` wire targets
   `wait.unit` only, a boundary adapter. An acceptance binds the unit, its revision, the graph's digest, the node's
   version, its inputs, its prerequisites and the attempt, and is invalidated transitively. Ports are references.
2. **Typed wires** (§E3): `after`, `data`, `wait`, `trigger`, `failed` and `rejected`, with `allOf` and `anyOf` groups
   (an `anyOf` keeps one witness), and no expressions. A cycle, a missing reference, an incompatible schema or a wire to
   another repository's node is refused.
3. **`quest.close`** (§E5): in a graph that holds it, it is the graph's end, so the quest is Done exactly when the work
   ends.
   - The agent's new `ready` records its result as a `Submitted` operation, and the agent's `done` is refused there.
   - The holds, the evidence, the departure's yes and the review move onto that submission (today Done-only:
     Quests.cs:144-192, QuestExchange.cs:1529-1540), so the landing waits on an unheld submission.
   - A chain advances on an unheld submission (where today Quests.cs:1125 waits for an unheld Done), and the close
     closes the chain's quests in order.
   - Daoris never writes Done before the end. The person's own done is an explicit override (the graph ends
     *overridden*, its pending nodes stopped), and only D69 can leave a chain *partially closed*, which is shown with
     its recovery.
   - `Submitted` is one of a take's own kinds, so it is refused on a lost take and lost with it on rebase (WAITCLAIM1-4).
     Its replacement follows operation order, and the successor it publishes is bound to it and seals it. The person's
     reopen withdraws or holds that successor, and a successor lost with its source has its session stopped.
   - The close is fenced across two stores: the unit's record lock held by the lease's loop across the whole close, and
     one SQLite transaction over every quest of the chain, rechecking each claim, current submission and hold.
   - It applies only to runs bound after the graph is set. This amends D46, D144 and D65's timing only in those
     graphs.
4. **`wait.unit`** (§E6), a waited node: local units through their adapter, and another repository's work only through
   an accepted quest commitment, read from the local projection. D79's session `wait` stays. A possible distributed
   wait is shown and recovered by the person; no universal deadlock prevention is promised.
5. **Workflows become graphs** (§E7): one model, bounded at 24 authored nodes (D157's `MaxSteps`) and 96 wires.
   - The exact decoding contract: an `all` becomes a join node keeping its identity and failure policy, a skip becomes
     `skipped`, and synthetic sinks, tasks and joins are counted apart.
   - An unsupported version stays kept and unreadable, and the original digest stays the identity, with deterministic
     decoding.
   - Current's graph is derived from the rules, the landing rule's `cycle` being its fragment.
6. **Chains are not wires** (§E8): CHAIN3's responses stay subscriptions. Setup's *Work* pages and D157's planned editor
   are one graph editor.
7. **The rows** (§E10): NODE1, WIRE1a (validation), WIRE1b (runtime evaluation), GRAPH1, WORKFLOW2 (D157's versions
   decoded), CLOSE1a (the submission and its holds, in the service) and CLOSE1b (the close's execution and recovery).
   The CLOSE rows are Codex's QUESTCLOSE1, renamed, since QUESTCLOSE1 is already the person's own done.
   - WAITUNIT1 joins the parallel gate after DELEGATE1.
   - GRAPHEDIT1 and GRAPHVIEW1 absorb CYCLE1b, WORKFLOW1g and the configured END4b; GRAPHVIEW1 follows WORKFLOW2.
   - WORKFLOW1j-1l's promises are each allocated a row and a proof.
   - Canaries 38-50 add migration equivalence, lost claims, closure deadlocks, witness invalidation, restart
     de-duplication, a stale binding, a claim race, holds before the landing, a chain under `ready`, skips and
     groups, and maximal and unreadable versions.

Codex reviewed the first draft. It found the changes to its proposal mostly improvements, and asked for a round
focused on closure and migration. Its re-check found the lifecycle, the distributed waits, the migration contract and
the rows resolved, and asked for one more round on closure: the override, chain atomicity, `Submitted`'s loss and
order, the successor's binding, and the close's fence. Canaries 51-56 hold those. Each point was checked against the
code and adopted.

Two questions go to the owner (§E11): what the asker sees (recommended: *submitted*, then the current gate or hold,
then *done*), and the names on the page (recommended: *node* and *wire*, 节点 and 连线).

**Note, 2026-10-17 (FABRIC-END3, the ref watch on link 1).** `Trees/RefWatch.cs` puts one `ChangeWatch` root on the common
git dir of each repository holding Daoris's work, and binds link 1 on its own chain. A hint judges that repository's ends
through `EndKeeping` (`SessionTrees.KeepEndsAsync`, the line read as the tidy reads it). The judge is handed the
repository alone and reads the refs afresh. Where it wrote a `HeldFact`, it emits `work.held`; links 2-4 stay unbound
there until END4a.

- **The filter** (`RefWatch.Hears`) keeps the design's final paths and drops every `.lock` and `.new`, the objects, the
  logs, the index, `FETCH_HEAD` and `ORIG_HEAD`. A deletion is a hint like any other.
- **The set** is the registry's repositories that `session-branches.json` or `landings.json` holds a standing entry of,
  where the root's `.git` is a folder (a main checkout, as the tidy asks). The common dir is that folder, so no process
  starts at an open. With no work kept, the registry is not asked. The set is read at the start and at the topic's whole
  deadline. A repository that no longer holds work is let go of (`ChangeWatch.Unwatch`), and a root the cap of 32 left on
  its deadline takes the place, opened and judged.
- **Daoris's own moves** call `RefMoves.Mine` once git is done: a tree opened (`SessionTrees.OpenAsync`, which also
  adds a repository not yet watched), *Accept* when it landed (`TreeLands.LandAsync`), and the tidy's removals
  (`SessionTrees.TidyEmptyAsync`). `ChangeWatch.Now` makes the repository due at once, with `why` `own`, and takes up
  the watcher's echo already heard, so the move is judged once (canary 11). A later echo writes nothing.
- **The reconciliation is the tidy's pass**, unchanged at its 5 min pace, which is the `refs` topic's deadline. A
  repository's own deadline reconciles its root (an absent folder opened, a replaced one reopened, each then judged)
  and judges nothing a second time. So the watch adds no git start at the pace: only a move costs a judgement. Recovery
  and bounds are EVENT2's, and two repositories are judged at once.
- **One keeper per repository at once** in a process: the tidy, *Accept* and the watch all keep ends, so `EndKeeping`
  holds a gate per home and repository.
- **The machine log** is `change.judged` on topic `refs`: the subject is the repository, `acted` is the facts written,
  and `waitedMs` plus `ms` is the delay from a ref move to its end. Link 1's `chain.fired` follows a write. The machine
  log design's row names `own`.
- **Measured once on this machine**, its antivirus on and the owner's install running (`RefWatchProcessTests`, one
  scratch repository under `_fixtures/`):
  - Each write was one judgement: a commit in a tree, its ref deleted and made again, a merge onto the line, a
    `pack-refs` and the tree's switch. Each began 24-254 ms after git exited.
  - The keeper itself took 417-4,071 ms per judgement (2,263 for the commit), so its git starts, not the watch, are the
    cost.
  - With 32 repositories watched, subfolders and all, the process held 30 more handles (0.94 each) and gave them back.
    Its nonpaged counter did not move (73,672 bytes before, open and after), where EVENT2 read about 1.3 KB a watcher:
    the counter's granularity, not a zero.
  - The merge wrote the branch's end. The deletion wrote none (a deletion alone is no end).
- **Proof:** `RefWatchTests` (fast) holds the filter, canaries 1-4 and 11 on git's measured sequences, the cap's place
  given back and the driver's set. `DriverWatchTopicsTests` holds a thousand ref hints through the loop running no look
  and no sync. `RefWatchProcessTests` (Process, serial for its timings) holds the real watcher and the numbers above.
- **Left:** canary 10's first day on the owner's use, read from the install's log once it carries this build: the
  `refs` lines' delay and their count per hour beside the tidy's. Auto-landing stays on the look (§B4, END7).

**Note, 2026-10-17 (FABRIC-MILESTONE1a, the default inventory derived and the milestone's answer).** The design's §D0 and §D3
for the default cycle, read-only. Nothing is stored, and no fact is written twice.

- **The inventory** (`Trees/Inventory/WorkInventory.cs`) is derived at each read from `landings.json`, `session-branches.json`
  (with END2's `Held`, `Unit`, `GoneAt` and traces), the trees and the folders under `trees/.tidied` (purge date: the move plus
  `BranchTidying.SetAsideFor`), the `refs/daoris/discarded/` refs, `sessions/auto-landings.json` and the session folders as
  evidence. `WorkInventory.Derive` is pure over `InventoryFacts`; `WorkInventories.GatherAsync` reads the home and git
  (`for-each-ref` only) and writes nothing. A unit is the session branch's `Unit` id; the landings join it by `Units`. A record
  from before units were kept is a unit named `branch:<repository>/<branch>` or `landing:<repository>/<branch>`.
- **Each entry** has a role (temporary, result, evidence, recovery) and a disposition (kept, retained until, removed with
  its proof, blocked with why, pending with what waits). The line the work reached is the result once the end is held; a landing
  branch the line does not hold is the result, and becomes temporary once it does. Moved-aside folders and recovery refs are
  recovery, retained: the folder until its purge date, the ref by its own rule.
- **The milestone** per unit: *end* from the kept `HeldFact` (`ended` with its proof, revision and the moment it was first
  held), else `handed` where a landing carries it, else `not-ended`; *result*; *cleanup*, `pending` (each wait named, a busy tree
  as the session holding it) or `completed` (what went, with proof); *recovery*. The end is **not judged again** here: a fact
  whose branch moved is cleared by the keeper's next look (§B5), so this reads what it last proved, and a look never ends work by
  a guess. `EndJudgement`'s proof codes are the words (`ProofSaid`).
- **An unknown owner blocks** (canary 27): a tree no record names, or a folder git cannot read a branch of, is a unit of its
  own with end `unknown`, `blocked`, saying so; a recovery ref no record names is retained, never guessed into a unit.
- **The doors.** `daoris-driver work state [<unit>] [--entries]` is new; `trees state [--entries]` with no name says the same, and
  `trees state <session|branch>` keeps its plugin ask and now says the unit's milestone first. A unit is named by its id, its
  session or either branch. The route `WORK_STATE` {optional `unit`} is **new**, classed **open** in the pipe census and a read
  that declares nothing (`StatePublisherTests`'s landing family); it is not a press, so the publishing table gains no row. The
  page is MILESTONE1b's.
- **Not read, and said.** Without the service the terminal reads no recovery refs and cannot tell a tree a session holds from
  an idle one, and says so; a repository with no checkout here lists no refs. The tidy's last guards (a locked tree, a hidden
  change) are the look's and not recorded, so a tree they keep reads as pending, not with their reason.
- **Proof:** `WorkInventoryTests` and `WorkWordsTests` (fast): the four milestones, each entry kind and disposition, old records,
  canary 27's derivation half, the gather writing nothing, and the host's wiring; `DriverModuleLinesTests` and
  `StatePublisherTests` for the route.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (FABRIC-MILESTONE1b, the milestone shown).** The page reads `WORK_STATE` through `useWorkState` (`bridge/lines.ts`)
and says the milestone in four places, each from the driver's answer and nothing judged again:

- **The work's foot:** `WorkMilestone` (End, Result, Clean-up, Recovery), above *How it came to be* in the attended session, for this
  machine's own record the driver names a unit for. **What needs you:** a row of kind `work` (group *waiting for your word*), only
  for a unit whose clean-up has a *blocked* entry or whose owner nothing records (`waitsOnPerson`); a wait the cycle ends by itself
  is no row. Its door is the session. It counts in the badge as every row does. **Branches:** a one-line `MilestoneLine` under each
  branch `WorkspaceTidied` lists, matched by repository and branch. **Ask Daoris:** `HelpRoomWork`, *Where work has got to*, in the
  terminal's own words (`WorkWords`), the units whose cleanup waits, whose owner is unknown or whose recovery is kept (ten at most),
  the rest counted, and what could not be read.
- **Names:** the labels are *End*, *Result*, *Clean-up* and *Recovery* (结束, 成果, 清理, 找回). The owner's plain pair *Done / 结束*
  stays for the graph's done: the glossary's `done` term avoids 结束 for a quest's 完成 (and `names:check --all` already reports
  `errors.HARNESS_ACTION_IDLE` for it), so the milestone says *End reached* rather than *Done*, to keep one word per meaning.
- **Words from the driver pass through as recorded** (an entry's `detail`, an end's `said`); a proof's code, an entry's kind and
  every label are the page's, in both languages.
- **Refetch:** no new scope. The milestone's key (`allWorkState`) is asked again with the `landing`, `sweep` and `sessions` scopes.
- **Ask Daoris's door:** `useWorkState` is a read, which `HelpCoverageTests` does not list (it lists what changes something), so it
  owes neither a door nor an exemption.
- **Proof:** `WorkMilestone.test.tsx`, `attention.test.ts`, `WorkspaceTidied.test.tsx` and `WorkFrame.test.tsx` (the foot), in both
  locales; `HelpRoomWorkTests` and the three room goldens.

**Note, 2026-10-17 (FABRIC-UNIT1a, the work-unit adapters).** The design's §D2, read-only: one projection for every kind of work, so
a later node (§E2) reads a branch, a quest and an ask the same way. Nothing is stored, and no adapter writes.

- **The shape** (`Trees/Inventory/WorkUnit.cs`): `WorkUnit { Id, Kind, Owner, Subject, End, Entries, Executors, Links }`, with
  `Results`, `Evidence` and `State` read from them.
  - `Id` is stable: the branch unit's id END2 writes (or the name MILESTONE1a gives older work), `quest:<id>`, `ask:<id>`.
  - `Owner { Repository, Workspace, Record, Writer }` names the canonical record and its one writer (§D2's table). `Record` is
    null where no record names the unit: canary 27's unknown owner, which never retires.
  - `Subject { Artifact, Revision }` is what an acceptance will bind (§E2): a branch and the tip its kept fact judged, or a
    quest and when it last moved. An adapter reads records, never git, so a revision no record holds is null.
  - `End` is MILESTONE1a's `MilestoneEnd`, in words every kind shares (`WorkEndState`). They add *dropped*, for a quest declined
    or an ask the person closed (§D3's "or the person drops it").
  - `Entries` are §D3's inventory. The evidence among them are pointers: a quest's or an ask's own record and its files by
    name, never a path.
  - `Executors` are the sessions, never owners (§D2).
  - `State` is *retired* only once the end is over, the owner known and every entry terminal (kept, retained or removed).
    Otherwise it is *open*.
  - Not built yet: `cycle`, which GRAPH1 gives, and `commitment`, which DELEGATE1 gives.
- **The adapters:**
  - **Branch** (`WorkInventory.Units`) is MILESTONE1a's inventory, re-expressed rather than derived a second time. It reads
    `session-branches.json` and `landings.json` (an older landing alone, or an auto-landing alone, names a unit of its own),
    with the trees, the folders moved aside, the recovery refs and the session folders, exactly as before. Its writer is the
    driver.
  - **Quest** (`Quests/QuestUnits.cs`) reads the service's answer of every quest. Its end is the status, never judged here:
    a done held for the person's yes or its evidence is no end yet, a decline drops it, and a status it does not know is
    *unknown*. The writer is the service.
  - **Ask** (`Quests/AskUnits.cs`) reads every ask. Its end is the state the service derives. The writer is the service.
- **Links, not copies** (`WorkLink { Relation, Unit }`, `WorkUnits.Linked`):
  - A session that served a quest worked the branch unit of its tree, so the unit is `produced-by` the quest and the quest
    `produced` it. The tree's entry, or the session branch its name makes, picks the unit; else the one unit naming the session.
  - A landing that carries one unit alone links it to the quest the landing names. One carrying several does not say which
    unit served the quest, so it links none: never a guess.
